1. Direct path: E → H (edges: (E, H))
2. Path via F and G: E → F → G → H (edges: (E, F), (F, G), (G, H))
3. Path via M and F: E → M → F → H (edges: (E, M), (M, F), (F, H))
4. Path via L and M: E → L → M → H (edges: (E, L), (L, M), (M, H))

These paths are edge-disjoint, meaning no two paths share an edge. By Menger's theorem, the minimal number of edges to remove is equal to the number of edge-disjoint paths, which is 4. 

To verify, we select one edge from each path:
- From path 1: (E, H)
- From path 2: (E, F) (or (F, G) or (G, H))
- From path 3: (E, M) (or (M, F) or (F, H))
- From path 4: (E, L) (or (L, M) or (M, H))

Removing these edges breaks all paths:
- Path 1 is broken by removing (E, H).
- Path 2 is broken by removing (E, F).
- Path 3 is broken by removing (E, M).
- Path 4 is broken by removing (E, L).

No smaller set of edges can disconnect E and H, as there are 4 edge-disjoint paths, requiring at least 4 edges to be removed.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(E, H), (E, F), (E, M), (E, L)]<|end_of_box|>
